Evaluating the Reasoning Abilities of LLMs on Underrepresented Mathematics
Competition Problems
SAMUEL GOLLADAY and MAJID BANI YAGHOUB, University of Missouri: Kansas City, USA
Understanding the limitations of Large Language Models (LLMs) in mathematical reasoning has been the focus of several recent
studies; however, the majority of these studies use the same datasets for benchmarking, which limits the generalizability of their
findings and may not fully capture the diverse challenges present in mathematical tasks. The purpose of the present study is to analyze
the performance of LLMs on underrepresented mathematics competition problems. We prompted three leading LLMs (GPT-4o-mini,
Gemini-2.0-Flash, and DeepSeek-V3) with the Missouri Collegiate Mathematics Competition problems in the areas of Calculus, Analytic
Geometry, and Discrete Mathematics. The LLM’s responses were then compared to the known correct solutions in order to determine
the accuracy of the LLM for each problem domain. We also analyzed the LLMs’ reasoning to explore patterns in errors across problem
types and models. DeepSeek-V3 has the best performance in all three categories of Calculus (80%), Analytic Geometry (80%), and
Discrete Mathematics (90%), both in reasoning and correct final answers. All three LLMs exhibited notably weak performance in
Geometry. The majority of errors made by DeepSeek-V3 were attributed to computational and logical mistakes, whereas GPT-4o-mini
frequently exhibited logical and approach-related errors. Gemini, on the other hand, tended to struggle with incomplete reasoning and
drawing rushed conclusions. In conclusion, evaluating LLMs on underrepresented mathematics competition datasets can provide
deeper insights into their distinct error patterns and highlight ongoing challenges in structured reasoning, particularly within the
domain of Geometry.
1
Introduction
Large Language Models (LLMs) have increasingly become popular in various fields, from research to everyday questions.
They have often demonstrated good performance in some fields of science and technology [9, 20, 21]. Nonetheless,
their ability to solve mathematical problems has become a question of considerable importance. Problems in Calculus,
Analytic Geometry, and Discrete Mathematics require a blend of abstract and logical reasoning, visualization, numerical
performance, and knowledge of problem-solving techniques [1, 7]. Problems in these fields form a metric for analyzing
LLM performance in logic-oriented problems [8, 10, 13].
Several studies have tested LLMs’ abilities to solve math problems [1, 3, 8, 13]; however, they have often limitations
as follows: (i) Most studies use the same datasets for benchmarking, such as the MATH and GSM8K datasets. This can
lead to dataset "contamination," where LLMs are trained on the same data used for evaluation. Such contamination
compromises the fairness and validity of the assessment and artificially inflates performance metrics, making it difficult
to accurately measure a model’s true capabilities; (ii) some studies focus exclusively on evaluating flagship language
models. While this is useful for understanding the current limits of LLM capabilities, it does not accurately reflect the
models that are accessible to the general public; (iii) other studies assess LLM performance solely based on the final
answer, paying little attention to the underlying reasoning process.
To address these issues, we evaluate the performance of freely accessible and currently prominent LLMs on underrep-
resented mathematics competition datasets, focusing not only on accuracy but also on the quality and structure of their
reasoning. Moreover, this study extends beyond overall accuracy metrics to provide a detailed analysis of the reasoning
Authors’ Contact Information: Samuel Golladay, sgwtd@umkc.edu; Majid Bani Yaghoub, baniyaghoubm@umkc.edu, University of Missouri: Kansas City,
Kansas City, Missouri, USA.
Manuscript submitted to ACM

arXiv:2512.24505v1 [cs.AI] 30 Dec 2025

errors each model exhibits. By identifying and characterizing these reasoning failures, we aim to uncover the specific
challenges LLMs face when solving complex, underrepresented mathematical problems and to better understand their
current limitations. This investigation addresses two central questions: (1) To what extent can LLMs reliably produce
correct solutions to complex, multi-step mathematical problems? and (2) Are the reasoning processes underpinning these
solutions mathematically valid? The findings offer a more nuanced understanding of LLMs’ mathematical capabilities
to solve underrepresented mathematical problems, contributing valuable insights into their potential use in education,
scientific research, and automated problem-solving systems [2, 6, 12].
2
Materials and Methods
In order to assess the performance of the LLMs in the three mathematical disciplines, we prompted the models with the
problem statements, as well as a command to solve the problem analytically.
2.1
Datasets
The Missouri Collegiate Mathematics Competition (MCMC) datasets are relatively unknown and, to the best of our
knowledge, have not been previously used to evaluate LLMs capabilities [16]. This significantly reduces the likelihood of
dataset contamination and allows for a more reliable assessment of model performance. The MCMC draws its problems
from a variety of reputable sources, including but not limited to the William Lowell Putnam Mathematical Competition,
the MAA (Mathematical Association of America) Problem Directory, the Mathematical Olympiads of the USSR, and the
Iowa Collegiate Mathematics Competition. The MCMC problems were categorized into three mathematical disciplines:
Calculus, Analytic Geometry, and Discrete Mathematics. Ten problems were chosen from each category, resulting in a
total of thirty problems. Additionally, problems were chosen in a way that they could be used for assessing the LLMs’
accuracy based on a final answer as well as the reasoning process. Hence, problems requiring mathematical proof were
not included in this study. The sets of selected problems with the specific year and correct answers are available in
Section 1 of the supplementary document.
2.2
LLMs Evaluated
The study assessed the performance of three prominent Large Language Models (LLMs): GPT-4o-mini, Gemini-2.0-flash,
and DeepSeek-V3. GPT-4o-mini is a lightweight variant of OpenAI’s GPT-4o, optimized for efficiency and speed [17, 19].
Gemini-2.0-flash, developed by Google, excels in natural language understanding and is also designed for efficiency
[5, 11]. DeepSeek is designed for technical tasks, particularly in mathematics, and is known for its ability to model
logical progression and identify mathematical patterns, as well as its enormously reduced training cost [4, 14]. All three
models were tested using the same set of problems from the Missouri Collegiate Mathematics Competition, with each
model prompted to provide both reasoning and final answers.
2.3
Evaluation Criteria
The models’ responses were categorized into four possible outcomes: (1) correct final answer with correct reasoning,
(2) incorrect final answer with correct reasoning (e.g., minor computational mistakes), (3) incorrect final answer
with incorrect reasoning, and (4) incorrect final answer with correct reasoning. We conducted a detailed analysis of
reasoning errors based on expert evaluation, categorizing responses with incorrect reasoning into 11 distinct types.
These reasoning error categories are summarized in Table 1.
2

Table 1. Categories of Reasoning Errors in Underrepresented Mathematical Problem Solving
Error Type
Description
Ambiguous Step
Steps that are unclear, imprecise, or open to multiple interpretations.
Debatable Theories
Use of theorems, principles, or methods with significant ambiguity or lacking consensus.
Incomplete Reasoning
Omission of necessary steps or failure to fully develop the solution.
Irrelevant Step
Inclusion of steps that do not contribute to solving the problem.
Logical Error
Reasoning that follows an illogical or invalid progression.
Computational Error
Miscalculation while evaluating expressions or performing operations.
Methodological Error
Incorrect application or selection of a mathematical method or technique.
Fact Error
Misuse or misstatement of mathematical facts, theorems, or definitions.
Approach Error
Adoption of an incorrect or inefficient strategy to solve the problem.
Redundancy Error
Inclusion of repetitive or unnecessary steps without no progress.
Rushed to Conclusion
Prematurely providing a final answer without sufficient reasoning.
3
Results
The performance of the evaluated LLMs in solving mathematics competition problems was assessed based on two main
criteria: the accuracy of the final answer and the correctness of the reasoning process. The output solutions provided by
GPT-4o-mini, Gemini-2.0-flash, and DeepSeek-V3 are available in sections 2-4 of the supplementary document.
3.1
Evaluation of Model Performances
Figure 1 provides a comparison of the three language models—GPT-4o-mini, Gemini 2.0 Flash, and DeepSeek V3—across
the three mathematical domains. Panel (a) summarizes the overall performance of each model in terms of the percentage
of correct answers with correct reasoning, incorrect answers with correct reasoning, and incorrect answers with
incorrect reasoning. Among the models, DeepSeek-V3 demonstrates the highest accuracy with the vast majority of its
answers classified as correct. Panels (b), (c), and (d) break down the types of reasoning errors observed in the calculus,
geometry, and discrete mathematics problems, respectively. Each panel shows only the error types that occurred with
non-zero frequency. The most prominent reasoning error across all three domains is the presence of logical errors.
In Calculus (Panel b), both GPT-4o-mini and Gemini exhibit a majority of logical mistakes, while DeepSeek mostly
suffers from computational errors. In Geometry (Panel c), logical and computational errors are common among all three
models. For Discrete Math (Panel d), all of DeepSeek-V3’s errors are due to logical reasoning mistakes, whereas Gemini
displays mostly logical errors and approach errors.
3.2
Assessment of Model Accuracies
Table 2 shows the classification of the models’ responses for each mathematical category. These outcomes reflect the
models’ ability to arrive at the correct answer and the logical soundness of their reasoning processes. There was no
case of an incorrect final answer with correct reasoning, possibly due to low sample size.

3.3
Analysis of Incorrect Reasoning
Table 3 provides a deeper look into the types of reasoning errors made by the models when their final answers were
incorrect. The table highlights the percentage of errors in each of the 11 reasoning categories: ambiguous steps, debatable
theories, incomplete reasoning, irrelevant steps, logical errors, computational errors, methodological errors, fact errors,
approach errors, redundancy errors, and rushed conclusions.

GPT-4o-mini primarily struggles with logical and approach errors across all mathematical domains, indicating a
tendency to follow flawed reasoning paths or select suboptimal problem-solving strategies. In Geometry, it also shows
a notable rate of incomplete reasoning, suggesting difficulties in carrying solutions through to completion. Gemini
exhibits inconsistent reasoning patterns, with significant incomplete reasoning and rushed conclusions in Geometry,
pointing to a tendency to jump to answers without fully developed steps. DeepSeek shows the highest frequency
of logical and computational errors, especially in Calculus and Discrete Math, reflecting issues with both reasoning
accuracy and numerical operations. Overall, each model reveals distinct weaknesses in how they process and execute
mathematical reasoning.
4
Discussion and Conclusion
The results presented in this study highlight the strengths and weaknesses of the three large language models
(LLMs)—GPT-4o-mini, Gemini-2.0-flash, and DeepSeek-V3—in solving underrepresented mathematical problems from
the following categories: Calculus, Analytic Geometry, and Discrete Mathematics.
Overall, the models performance varied significantly in providing correct final answers. DeepSeek-V3 exhibited the
highest accuracy in both final answers and reasoning, with particularly impressive results in discrete mathematics, where
it achieved the highest percentage of correct answers with correct reasoning (90%). This indicates that DeepSeek-V3
may have a better understanding of the mathematical principles involved in discrete mathematics problems compared
to the other models. In contrast, GPT-4o-mini performed poorly across all three problem domains, receiving a maximum
percentage of correct answers with correct reasoning of 30%. While previous studies have shown that GPT-4o-mini can
successfully solve simple math problems, GPT-4o-mini’s performance in this study demonstrates its inability to solve
more complex problems [1, 2, 6]. Gemini 2.0-flash demonstrated mediocre performance, with it solving 70% of discrete
math problems, but only 50% of geometry problems. This highlights the limitations of Gemini 2.0-flash in visualizing
abstract geometric figures.
5

When analyzing the incorrect reasoning, the most frequent errors vary by model and mathematical domain. However,
in general the most common errors were logical errors and errors due to incomplete reasoning. Additionally, many of
the incomplete reasoning errors involved models failing to test all cases. That is, a model would check one or two cases
and then assume that a condition would hold for all possible cases. Additionally, logical errors were typically related to
steps being performed incorrectly. The prevalence of these errors demonstrates that large language models currently
struggle to understand individual steps in the grand scheme of the entire problem, and rather begin a step, or a case,
and then upon completion determine that the problem is finished. However, DeepSeek-V3 had the fewest errors in
any problem domain, likely due to the fact that it double-checks its solution before finishing. This gave it an edge on
problems requiring multiple cases to be checked.
The findings underscore the need for continued improvements in LLMs’ reasoning capabilities in mathematics.
Although sample size is a limitation, we are 95% confident that the true accuracy of DeepSeek-V3, Gemini-2.0-flash, and
GPT-4o-mini lie within (69%, 97%), (42%, 78%), and (8%, 38%) respectively. As such, it is clear that these models have
room for improvement. Gemini-2.0-flash demonstrated unreliable performance, showing that its responses to complex,
multi-step mathematical problems should require verification. Finally, GPT-4o-mini’s poor performance across all three
mathematical domains showcases its inability to solve complex underrepresented mathematical problems and thus
perhaps worse overall performance in logic-oriented tasks.
Other prominent LLMs to be tested include Meta’s LLaMa 3 family as well as more advanced variants of Gemini and
GPT 4o. The models tested in this study were chosen specifically due to their free-to-use nature and current prominence.
Additionally, research into other novel methods of improving LLM performance, such as proxies that offload compu-
tation to improve computational accuracy, is ongoing [15]. Another crucial element of evaluating LLM performance
is using less common datasets for benchmarking [3]. Using underrepresented datasets prevents contamination and
prevents LLMs from appearing overly accurate when performance testing [18].
In conclusion, while LLMs have made significant strides in solving mathematical problems, there are still notable areas,
such as Geometry, where their reasoning needs improvement. Models such as DeepSeek-V3 show promise in handling
underrepresented, multi-step mathematical tasks, but other prevalent models (e.g., GPT-4o-mini and Gemini-2.0-flash)
still exhibit limitations in terms of logical consistency and computational accuracy. As these models continue to evolve,
it will be important to address these challenges to improve their overall reliability in mathematical problem-solving.
Future research should focus on refining reasoning processes, reducing errors, and expanding the models’ understanding
of the process of solving multi-step problems with greater consistency and accuracy.

A.1.4
1999 Session 2: 2. Give a precise characterization of those points in the plane which do not lie on a tangent line
to the curve 𝑦= 𝑥4 −6𝑥2. Solution. Given a point (𝑡,𝑡4 −6𝑡2) on the curve, the equation of the tangent line at that
point is 𝑦= (4𝑡3 −12𝑡)𝑥−(3𝑡4 −6𝑡2). For a fixed value of 𝑥, as 𝑡varies, the maximum value of 𝑦will occur when

This polynomial factors as (12𝑡2 −12)(𝑥−𝑡), so the critical values occur at 𝑡= 1, −1,𝑥. The corresponding 𝑦values
are −8𝑥+ 3, 8𝑥+ 3, and 𝑥4 −6𝑥2 and a point (𝑥,𝑦) will not lie on any tangent line if the 𝑦coordinate is greater than
the maximum of these three quantities. More specifically, the set of points which lie on no tangent line to the curve
is {(𝑥,𝑦) | |𝑥| > 3 and 𝑦> 𝑥4 −6𝑥2, or |𝑥| ≤3, and 𝑦> 8|𝑥| + 3}. Note that 𝑦= ±8𝑥+ 3 are the tangent lines at the
points of inflection, (∓1, −5), and that (±3, 27) are where these tangents intersect the quartic.

A.2.7
2001 Session 2: 3. A convex decagon and all of its diagonals are drawn. How many interior points of intersection
of the diagonals are there, if it is assumed that no 3 diagonals share a common interior point? Solution. Let 𝑁(𝑛) be the
number of such interior intersection points in a convex 𝑛-gon. Label the vertices 1, 2, 3, . . ., 𝑛. Any four labels selected
from this list (e.g., (1, 3,𝑛, 6)) define a quadrilateral, which is convex because the 𝑛-gon is convex. The quadruple of
integers therefore also defines uniquely an interior intersection point of the diagonals of the quadrilateral, and hence
also of the 𝑛-gon.
The total number of interior intersection points of diagonals of the 𝑛-gon (assuming no 3 of the diagonals meet at a
common point) is thus the number of ways that four integers can be selected (without regard to order) from a list of 𝑛
distinct integers. Therefore,
𝑁(𝑛) =
𝑛
4

= 𝑛(𝑛−1)(𝑛−2)(𝑛−3)
24
,
which for the decagon gives 𝑁(10) = 210.

A.2.10
2007 Session 2: 2. Find the point on a given line such that the sum of its distances from two fixed points is a
minimum. Assume the two fixed points and the given line are in the same plane. Solution. If one of the two points is on
the line, then the solution is clearly that point. It is also clear that if the two points are on opposite sides of the line,
then the solution is the intersection of the line with the segment connecting the points. Now assume the two points
are on the same side of the line. Because we are interested in the sum of distances from two fixed points, consider the
family of ellipses with foci at the two points, and denote the foci 𝑃1 and 𝑃2. If an ellipse in this family intersects the line
in two points, say 𝐴and 𝐵, then from the definition of an ellipse, the sum of the distances from 𝐴to the foci equals the
sum of the distances from 𝐵to the foci. If a smaller ellipse in the family also intersects the line in two points, the same
thing happens, except that the sum of the distances is less for the smaller ellipse. Therefore, if we continue to shrink
the ellipse until it is tangent to the line, the point of tangency will be where the required minimum occurs.

be -1. The minimum 𝑆𝑛is −𝑛/2
Case 2. 𝑛is odd. 𝑆𝑛can be minimized by choosing (𝑛−1)/2 of the 𝑥𝑖’s to be 1 and (𝑛−1)/2 of the 𝑥𝑖’s to be -1. The

A.3.2
1997 Session 2: 1. Find positive integers 𝑛and 𝑎1,𝑎2, . . . ,𝑎𝑛such that
𝑎1 + 𝑎2 + · · · + 𝑎𝑛= 1997
and the product 𝑎1𝑎2 · · ·𝑎𝑛is as large as possible. Solution. We see that 𝑛= 666 and that all but one of the 𝑎𝑖equal 3
and the exceptional 𝑎𝑖is a 2 as follows. No 𝑎𝑖can be greater than 4 since one could increase the product by replacing
5 by 2 · 3, 6 by 3 · 3, 7 by 3 · 4, etc. There cannot be both a 2 and a 4 or three 2’s among the 𝑎𝑖since 2 · 4 < 3 · 3 and
2 · 2 · 2 < 3 · 3. Also, there cannot be two 4’s since 4 · 4 < 2 · 3 · 3. Clearly, no 𝑎𝑖is a 1. Hence the 𝑎𝑖are 3’s except possibly
for a 4 or for a 2 or for two 2’s. Since 1997 = 3 · 665 + 2, the only exception is a 2 and 𝑛= 666.

A.3.7
2001 Session 1: 2. Let {𝑥𝑖} denote any finite sequence with the following properties:

2002 Session 1: 5 Incorrect Reasoning, Incorrect Final Answer. We are tasked with finding the equations of the
boundary curves and the area of the flattened face of a solid formed by the intersection of two right circular cylinders
with radius 𝑟at right angles.
Problem Breakdown.
Understanding the Geometry. We have two cylinders, both with radius 𝑟, intersecting at right angles. Let’s assume the
cylinders are aligned along the 𝑥- and 𝑦-axes in 3D space. The equation of the first cylinder, which is aligned along the
𝑥-axis, is:
𝑦2 + 𝑧2 = 𝑟2
The equation of the second cylinder, aligned along the 𝑦-axis, is:
𝑥2 + 𝑧2 = 𝑟2
These two cylinders intersect in a shape known as a Bicylinder.
Flattening One of the Curved Faces. We need to imagine one of the curved faces of the intersection being "unwrapped"
or "flattened" onto a plane. We will consider the face corresponding to the portion of the first cylinder 𝑦2 + 𝑧2 = 𝑟2
in the region where the second cylinder intersects it. To do this, we will work in the 𝑦𝑧-plane, and observe that the
boundary curve of this face will be part of the intersection of the two cylinders. By substituting the equation of the
second cylinder 𝑥2 + 𝑧2 = 𝑟2 into the first cylinder’s equation, we will determine the boundary of the flattened face.
Step 1: Find the Equations of the Boundary Curves. The boundary of the face we are flattening corresponds to the
projection of the curve of intersection onto the 𝑦𝑧-plane. From the geometry of the cylinders, we can see that the
boundary of the curved face involves the intersection of the two cylinder equations. We already have the two cylinder
equations:
𝑦2 + 𝑧2 = 𝑟2
(cylinder along the 𝑥-axis)
𝑥2 + 𝑧2 = 𝑟2
(cylinder along the 𝑦-axis)
At the boundary of the flattened face, we are dealing with the region where 𝑥= 0, since this is the part of the intersection
of the cylinders projected onto the 𝑦𝑧-plane.
When 𝑥= 0, the second cylinder’s equation becomes:
𝑧2 = 𝑟2
⇒
𝑧= ±𝑟
This gives the boundary for 𝑧.
Substituting this into the first cylinder’s equation, we get:
𝑦2 + 𝑟2 = 𝑟2
⇒
𝑦= 0
34

Thus, the boundary curve of the flattened face lies along the lines where 𝑦= 0 and 𝑧= ±𝑟.
Step 2: Find the Area of the Flattened Face. The area of the flattened face is simply the area of a quarter circle, because
the boundary corresponds to a quarter of a circle with radius 𝑟.
The area of a full circle is 𝜋𝑟2. Since we have a quarter circle, the area is:
Area = 1
4𝜋𝑟2
Final Answer. The equations of the boundary curves of the flattened face are 𝑦= 0 and 𝑧= ±𝑟. The area of the flattened
face is 1
4𝜋𝑟2.

Step 1: Understanding the Problem. We are given a convex decagon, which has 10 sides, and all of its diagonals are
drawn. The problem specifies that no three diagonals share a common interior point. This means that each interior
intersection is formed by exactly two diagonals intersecting inside the decagon.
Step 2: Determining the Number of Diagonals. For any convex polygon with 𝑛sides, the number of diagonals can be
calculated using the formula:
Number of diagonals = 𝑛(𝑛−3)
2
For a decagon, where 𝑛= 10:
Number of diagonals = 10(10 −3)
2
= 10 × 7
2
= 35
Thus, there are 35 diagonals in the decagon.
Step 3: Finding the Number of Interior Intersection Points. The number of interior points of intersection of the diagonals
in a convex polygon is given by the combination formula for choosing 4 vertices from the 𝑛vertices of the polygon.
This is because each interior intersection point is formed by the diagonals connecting four distinct vertices.
46

The number of ways to choose 4 vertices from 10 vertices is:
10
4

= 10 × 9 × 8 × 7
4 × 3 × 2 × 1 = 210
Thus, the number of interior points of intersection of the diagonals in a convex decagon is 210 .

2006 Session 1: 4 Incorrect Reasoning, Incorrect Final Answer. We are tasked with finding all positive integers 𝑐
such that 𝑛(𝑛+𝑐) is never a perfect square for any positive integer 𝑛. To solve this problem, we begin by expressing the
given equation:
𝑛(𝑛+ 𝑐) = 𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛.
We want to determine for which values of 𝑐, this expression is never a perfect square for any positive integer 𝑛.
Suppose 𝑛(𝑛+ 𝑐) is a perfect square for some integer 𝑛. Then we have:
𝑛(𝑛+ 𝑐) = 𝑘2
for some integer 𝑘.
This leads to the equation:
𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛= 𝑘2.
We treat this as a quadratic equation in 𝑛:
𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛−𝑘2 = 0.
55

The discriminant of this quadratic equation is:
Δ = 𝑐2 + 4𝑘2.
For 𝑛to be an integer, the discriminant must be a perfect square. Let’s set the discriminant equal to another perfect
square, say 𝑚2, so we have:
𝑐2 + 4𝑘2 = 𝑚2.
This is a Diophantine equation of the form 𝑚2 −4𝑘2 = 𝑐2, which is a difference of squares:
(𝑚−2𝑘)(𝑚+ 2𝑘) = 𝑐2.
Now, let’s consider the case where 𝑐= 4. Substituting 𝑐= 4 into the equation 𝑛(𝑛+ 𝑐) = 𝑛2 + 4𝑛:
𝑛2 + 4𝑛= 𝑘2.
Rewriting this as:
𝑛2 + 4𝑛−𝑘2 = 0,
the discriminant becomes:
Δ = 42 + 4𝑘2 = 16 + 4𝑘2 = 4(𝑘2 + 4).
For 𝑛to be an integer, the discriminant must be a perfect square, so we need:
𝑘2 + 4 = 𝑚2
for some integer 𝑚. This is a difference of squares:
𝑚2 −𝑘2 = 4,
which factors as:
(𝑚−𝑘)(𝑚+ 𝑘) = 4.
The integer factor pairs of 4 are (1, 4), (−1, −4), (2, 2), and (−2, −2). Solving each pair gives:
- 𝑚−𝑘= 1 and 𝑚+ 𝑘= 4 gives 𝑚= 5
2, which is not an integer. - 𝑚−𝑘= −1 and 𝑚+ 𝑘= −4 gives 𝑚= −5
2 , which is
not an integer. - 𝑚−𝑘= 2 and 𝑚+ 𝑘= 2 gives 𝑚= 2 and 𝑘= 0. - 𝑚−𝑘= −2 and 𝑚+ 𝑘= −2 gives 𝑚= −2 and 𝑘= 0.
Thus, the only solution is 𝑘= 0, which implies:
𝑛2 + 4𝑛= 0
⇒
𝑛(𝑛+ 4) = 0.
This only has the solution 𝑛= 0, which is not a positive integer. Therefore, 𝑛(𝑛+ 4) cannot be a perfect square for
any positive integer 𝑛.
Thus, the only solution is:
56

𝑐= 4 .

2007 Session 2: 4 Correct Reasoning, Correct Final Answer. We are given the set
𝑆= {5𝑎+ 503𝑏| 𝑎,𝑏∈Z≥0},
and we are tasked with finding the largest integer that does not belong to 𝑆.
This is a classic application of the Frobenius coin problem. For two coprime integers 𝑚and 𝑛, the largest integer that
cannot be expressed as a nonnegative linear combination of 𝑚and 𝑛is given by the formula:
𝑔(𝑚,𝑛) = 𝑚𝑛−𝑚−𝑛.
In this case, we have 𝑚= 5 and 𝑛= 503, which are coprime. Applying the formula, we compute:
𝑔(5, 503) = 5 × 503 −5 −503 = 2515 −5 −503 = 2007.
Thus, the largest integer that does not belong to 𝑆is 2007 .

|

C.2.7
2001 Session 2: 3 Correct Reasoning, Correct Final Answer. Problem: A convex decagon and all of its diagonals
are drawn. How many interior points of intersection of the diagonals are there, if it is assumed that no 3 diagonals
share a common interior point?
Solution:
To form an intersection point inside the decagon, we need to choose four vertices. Each set of four vertices uniquely
determines two intersecting diagonals.
The number of ways to choose four vertices from the ten vertices of the decagon is given by the combination formula:
𝑛
𝑘

=
𝑛!
𝑘!(𝑛−𝑘)!
In our case, 𝑛= 10 (number of vertices) and 𝑘= 4 (number of vertices to choose). So, the number of ways to choose
four vertices is:
10
4

=
10!
4!(10 −4)! = 10!
4!6! = 10 × 9 × 8 × 7
4 × 3 × 2 × 1 = 10 × 3 × 7 = 210
Since each set of four vertices uniquely determines an interior intersection point, the number of interior intersection
points is equal to the number of ways to choose four vertices.
Therefore, there are 210 interior intersection points.
69

2007 Session 1: 4 Correct Reasoning, Correct Final Answer.
Problem Statement. Let 𝐴= (𝑥𝐴,𝑦𝐴) and 𝐵= (𝑥𝐵,𝑦𝐵) be two fixed points in the plane, and let the given line 𝐿be

Conclusion. The point 𝑃on the line 𝐿that minimizes the sum of distances 𝑃𝐴+ 𝑃𝐵is found by reflecting one of the
points across the line 𝐿and finding the intersection of the reflected point and the other point with the line 𝐿, when the

1997 Session 2: 1 Correct Reasoning, Correct Final Answer.
Problem Statement. Find positive integers 𝑛and 𝑎1,𝑎2, . . . ,𝑎𝑛such that
𝑎1 + 𝑎2 + · · · + 𝑎𝑛= 1997
and the product 𝑎1𝑎2 · · ·𝑎𝑛is as large as possible.
Solution.
Step 1: Understanding the Problem. We need to partition the number 1997 into positive integers 𝑎1,𝑎2, . . . ,𝑎𝑛such that
their sum is 1997 and their product is maximized.
Step 2: Optimal Partition Strategy. It is known that for a given sum, the product of numbers is maximized when the
numbers are as close to 𝑒≈2.718 as possible. Among integers, 3 is the closest to 𝑒. Therefore, we should use as many 3s
as possible.
Step 3: Applying the Strategy. Divide 1997 by 3:
1997 = 3 × 665 + 2
This gives us 665 numbers of 3 and a remainder of 2.
Step 4: Handling the Remainder. The remainder is 2, which is less than 3. Since 2 is a positive integer, we can include it
as is.
If the remainder were 1, it would be better to adjust one of the 3s and the remainder into two 2s because 3 + 1 = 2 + 2
and 3 × 1 = 3 < 2 × 2 = 4. However, in this case, the remainder is 2, so no adjustment is needed.
Step 5: Final Partition. Thus, the optimal partition is:
𝑛= 666 numbers: 665 threes and one two.
Step 6: Verification. Any other combination, such as using fewer threes and more twos or larger numbers, leads to a
product that is either equal or smaller. For example:
• Replacing a 3 and a 2 with two 2s and a 1 (but 1 reduces the product).
• Using larger numbers like 4, which can be expressed as 2 + 2, but the product remains the same (4 = 2 × 2).
Therefore, the maximum product is achieved with 665 threes and one two.
Final Answer. The positive integers are:
𝑛= 666
𝑎1 = 𝑎2 = · · · = 𝑎665 = 3
𝑎666 = 2
73

The maximum product is:
3665 × 2

such that:
(1) The sequence starts and ends with 𝑎.
(2) No two adjacent letters are the same.
Approach. We can model this problem using recurrence relations. Let’s define:
• 𝐴(𝑛): Number of valid sequences of length 𝑛ending with 𝑎.
• 𝐵(𝑛): Number of valid sequences of length 𝑛ending with 𝑏.
76

• 𝐶(𝑛): Number of valid sequences of length 𝑛ending with 𝑐.

Base Case. For 𝑛= 1, the only valid sequence is 𝑎:
𝐴(1) = 1,
𝐵(1) = 𝐶(1) = 0
Recurrence Relations. For 𝑛> 1, the recurrence relations are:
𝐴(𝑛) = 𝐵(𝑛−1) + 𝐶(𝑛−1)
𝐵(𝑛) = 𝐴(𝑛−1) + 𝐶(𝑛−1)

𝐴(𝑛) = 2𝐵(𝑛−1)
𝐵(𝑛) = 𝐴(𝑛−1) + 𝐵(𝑛−1)
Solving the Recurrence. We observe that 𝐴(𝑛) follows the recurrence:

The characteristic equation is:
𝑟2 −𝑟−2 = 0
with roots 𝑟= 2 and 𝑟= −1.

Problem Statement. Find all positive integers 𝑐such that 𝑛(𝑛+ 𝑐) is never a perfect square for any positive integer 𝑛.
Solution. We need to find all positive integers 𝑐for which the expression 𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛is never a perfect square for any
positive integer 𝑛.
Initial Analysis. Consider the equation:
𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛= 𝑘2
for some integer 𝑘≥1. We can rewrite this as:
𝑘2 = 𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛
𝑘2 −𝑛2 = 𝑐𝑛
(𝑘−𝑛)(𝑘+ 𝑛) = 𝑐𝑛
Approach. For fixed 𝑐, we need to determine whether there exist positive integers 𝑛and 𝑘satisfying this equation.
Parametric Solution. Let us set 𝑘= 𝑛+ 𝑡where 𝑡is a non-negative integer. Substituting:
(𝑛+ 𝑡)2 = 𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛
𝑛2 + 2𝑡𝑛+ 𝑡2 = 𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛
2𝑡𝑛+ 𝑡2 = 𝑐𝑛
𝑡2 = 𝑛(𝑐−2𝑡)
80

This implies that 𝑐−2𝑡must be a positive divisor of 𝑡2, and 𝑛=
𝑡2
𝑐−2𝑡must be a positive integer.
Special Cases.
Case 𝑐= 1. The equation becomes:
𝑡2 = 𝑛(1 −2𝑡)
For 𝑡≥1, the right side becomes non-positive while the left side is positive. Thus, the only possibility is 𝑡= 0, which
gives 𝑛= 0 - invalid. Therefore, no solutions exist for 𝑐= 1.
Case 𝑐= 2. The equation becomes:
𝑡2 = 𝑛(2 −2𝑡)
For 𝑡= 0: 𝑛= 0 (invalid) For 𝑡= 1: 1 = 𝑛(0) (no solution) For 𝑡≥1: The right side is non-positive. Thus, no solutions
exist for 𝑐= 2.
Case 𝑐= 4. The equation becomes:
𝑡2 = 𝑛(4 −2𝑡)
Testing small values:
• 𝑡= 0: 𝑛= 0 (invalid)
• 𝑡= 1: 1 = 𝑛(2) ⇒𝑛= 1
2 (not integer)
• 𝑡= 2: 4 = 𝑛(0) (no solution)
• 𝑡≥2: The denominator becomes zero or negative
Thus, no solutions exist for 𝑐= 4.
Verification of Other Values. For 𝑐= 3, we find a solution at 𝑛= 1:
1(1 + 3) = 4 = 22
Thus, 𝑐= 3 is excluded.
For 𝑐= 5, 𝑛= 4 gives:
4(4 + 5) = 36 = 62
Thus, 𝑐= 5 is excluded.
This pattern suggests that only 𝑐= 1, 2, 4 satisfy the condition.
General Proof. To prove that these are the only values, consider:
(1) For 𝑐≥5, we can often find solutions:
• For odd 𝑐, take 𝑛= (𝑐−1)2
4
• For even 𝑐, solutions frequently exist
(2) The cases 𝑐= 1, 2, 4 are special because:
• The equation 𝑡2 = 𝑛(𝑐−2𝑡) has no positive integer solutions
• The quadratic forms don’t permit perfect square solutions

Problem Statement. Let
𝑆= {5𝑎+ 503𝑏| 𝑎and 𝑏are nonnegative integers}.
What is the largest integer which does NOT belong to 𝑆?
Solution.
Understanding the Problem. The set 𝑆consists of all numbers expressible as 5𝑎+ 503𝑏where 𝑎and 𝑏are nonnegative
integers. We need to find the largest integer not in 𝑆.
Coin Problem Analogy. This problem resembles the Coin Problem, which seeks the largest monetary amount that
cannot be obtained using any combination of coins of given denominations. For two coprime integers 𝑥and 𝑦, the
largest number not expressible as 𝑚𝑥+ 𝑛𝑦is known as the Frobenius number, given by:
𝑔(𝑥,𝑦) = 𝑥𝑦−𝑥−𝑦.
Checking Coprimality. First, verify that gcd(5, 503) = 1. Since 503 is not divisible by 5, they are indeed coprime.
Applying the Frobenius Number Formula. Given that 5 and 503 are coprime, the largest number not in 𝑆is:
𝑔(5, 503) = 5 × 503 −5 −503 = 2515 −508 = 2007.
Verification. To ensure accuracy, let’s verify:
• Testing 2007: No nonnegative integers 𝑎,𝑏satisfy 5𝑎+ 503𝑏= 2007.
• Testing 2008: 5 × 301 + 503 × 1 = 1505 + 503 = 2008. Thus, 2008 is in 𝑆.
• Testing 2009: 5 × 100 + 503 × 3 = 500 + 1509 = 2009. Thus, 2009 is in 𝑆.
Conclusion. The largest integer not expressible as 5𝑎+ 503𝑏with nonnegative integers 𝑎,𝑏is indeed 2007 .

2002 Session 1: 5 Correct Reasoning, Correct Final Answer.
Problem Solution.
Problem Statement. Two right circular cylinders of radius 𝑟intersect at right angles to form a solid. This solid has four
curved faces. Imagine one of these faces “rolled out flat”. Find equations of the boundary curves of this flattened face
and also find its area.
Solution.
Setup. Consider two cylinders:
• Cylinder 1: 𝑥2 + 𝑦2 = 𝑟2 (aligned along the 𝑧-axis).
• Cylinder 2: 𝑦2 + 𝑧2 = 𝑟2 (aligned along the 𝑥-axis).
89

Their intersection forms a bicylinder with four curved faces. We focus on one face, part of Cylinder 1 within Cylinder
2.
Parameterizing the Face. For Cylinder 1, use cylindrical coordinates:

D.2.6
2001 Session 2: 1 Incorrect Reasoning, Incorrect Final Answer.
Problem Statement. Circle 𝐵lies wholly in the interior of circle 𝐴. Find the loci of points equidistant from the two
circles.
Solution.
Understanding the Problem. We have two circles:
• Circle 𝐴with center 𝑂and radius 𝑅.
• Circle 𝐵with center 𝑃and radius 𝑟, where 𝑟< 𝑅and 𝐵is entirely inside 𝐴.
We need to find all points 𝑄that are equidistant to both circles.
Distance to a Circle. The distance from a point 𝑄to a circle is defined as follows:
101

• If 𝑄is outside the circle, the distance is 𝑑(𝑄, center) −radius.
• If 𝑄is inside the circle, the distance is radius −𝑑(𝑄, center).
Case Analysis.
Case 1: Points Outside Both Circles. For 𝑄outside both 𝐴and 𝐵:
𝑑(𝑄,𝑂) −𝑅= 𝑑(𝑄, 𝑃) −𝑟
𝑑(𝑄,𝑂) −𝑑(𝑄, 𝑃) = 𝑅−𝑟
This is the equation of a hyperbola with foci 𝑂and 𝑃, and constant difference 𝑅−𝑟.
Case 2: Points Inside Both Circles. For 𝑄inside both 𝐴and 𝐵:
𝑅−𝑑(𝑄,𝑂) = 𝑟−𝑑(𝑄, 𝑃)
𝑑(𝑄,𝑂) −𝑑(𝑄, 𝑃) = 𝑅−𝑟
Again, a hyperbola with the same parameters.
Case 3: Points Inside 𝐴but Outside 𝐵. For 𝑄inside 𝐴but outside 𝐵:
𝑅−𝑑(𝑄,𝑂) = 𝑑(𝑄, 𝑃) −𝑟
𝑑(𝑄,𝑂) + 𝑑(𝑄, 𝑃) = 𝑅+ 𝑟
This is the equation of an ellipse with foci 𝑂and 𝑃, and constant sum 𝑅+ 𝑟.
Conclusion. The loci of points equidistant to both circles consist of:
(1) A hyperbola defined by 𝑑(𝑄,𝑂) −𝑑(𝑄, 𝑃) = 𝑅−𝑟for points outside both or inside both circles.
(2) An ellipse defined by 𝑑(𝑄,𝑂) + 𝑑(𝑄, 𝑃) = 𝑅+ 𝑟for points inside 𝐴but outside 𝐵.
D.2.7
2001 Session 2: 3 Correct Reasoning, Correct Final Answer.
Problem Statement. A convex decagon and all of its diagonals are drawn. How many interior points of intersection of
the diagonals are there, if it is assumed that no 3 diagonals share a common interior point?
Solution.
Understanding the Problem. We need to find the number of interior points where two diagonals of a convex decagon
intersect, under the condition that no three diagonals meet at a single interior point.
Approach. In a convex 𝑛-gon, any four distinct vertices define a quadrilateral. The two diagonals of this quadrilateral
intersect at a unique point inside the polygon. Since no three diagonals are concurrent, each set of four vertices
corresponds to exactly one unique intersection point.
Calculation. The number of ways to choose 4 vertices from 10 is given by the combination formula:
𝐶(𝑛,𝑘) =
𝑛!
𝑘!(𝑛−𝑘)!
102

For 𝑛= 10 and 𝑘= 4:
𝐶(10, 4) = 10!
4!6! = 210
Verification. For a convex quadrilateral (𝑛= 4):
𝐶(4, 4) = 1
This matches since there’s exactly one intersection point.
For a convex pentagon (𝑛= 5):
𝐶(5, 4) = 5
However, in a regular pentagon, all quadrilaterals formed by any four vertices have diagonals intersecting at the same
central point. But since the problem specifies no three diagonals meet at a common interior point, the scenario changes.
The general formula 𝐶(𝑛, 4) holds for 𝑛≥4 under the given condition.
Conclusion. Thus, for a convex decagon, the number of interior intersection points of the diagonals is:
210

D.2.10
2007 Session 2: 2 Correct Reasoning, Incorrect Final Answer.
Problem Statement. Find the point on a given line such that the sum of its distances from two fixed points is a minimum.
Assume the two fixed points and the given line are in the same plane.
Solution.
105

Approach. To find the point 𝑃on the line ℓthat minimizes the sum 𝑃𝐴+ 𝑃𝐵, where 𝐴and 𝐵are two fixed points, we
use the method of reflection.
Steps.
(1) Reflect one of the points (say 𝐵) across the line ℓto obtain 𝐵′. This ensures that for any point 𝑃on ℓ, 𝑃𝐵= 𝑃𝐵′.
(2) The sum 𝑃𝐴+ 𝑃𝐵then becomes 𝑃𝐴+ 𝑃𝐵′.
(3) The minimal sum 𝑃𝐴+𝑃𝐵′ is achieved when 𝑃lies on the straight line joining 𝐴and 𝐵′. Thus, 𝑃is the intersection
point of ℓand the line 𝐴𝐵′.
Verification. For any other point 𝑃′ on ℓ, the sum 𝑃′𝐴+𝑃′𝐵= 𝑃′𝐴+𝑃′𝐵′ is greater than 𝐴𝐵′ (by the triangle inequality),
unless 𝑃′ = 𝑃. Therefore, 𝑃is indeed the minimizing point.
Special Cases.
• If 𝐴and 𝐵are on the same side of ℓ, the reflection method still applies.
• If 𝐴or 𝐵lies on ℓ, then 𝑃coincides with that point, as the sum 𝑃𝐴+ 𝑃𝐵is minimized there.
Illustration.

𝑃𝐵
Conclusion. The point 𝑃on the line ℓthat minimizes the sum 𝑃𝐴+ 𝑃𝐵is the intersection point of ℓand the line joining
𝐴to the reflection 𝐵′ of 𝐵across ℓ.

D.3.2
1997 Session 2: 1 Correct Reasoning, Correct Final Answer.
Problem Statement. Find positive integers 𝑛and 𝑎1,𝑎2, . . . ,𝑎𝑛such that
𝑎1 + 𝑎2 + · · · + 𝑎𝑛= 1997
and the product 𝑎1𝑎2 · · ·𝑎𝑛is as large as possible.
Solution.
Step 1: Understanding the Problem. We need to partition the number 1997 into positive integers 𝑎1,𝑎2, . . . ,𝑎𝑛such that
their sum is 1997 and their product is maximized.
107

Step 2: Optimal Partition Strategy. It is known that for a given sum, the product of numbers is maximized when the
numbers are as close to 𝑒≈2.718 as possible. Among integers, 3 is the closest to 𝑒. Therefore, we should use as many 3s
as possible.
Step 3: Applying the Strategy. Divide 1997 by 3:
1997 = 3 × 665 + 2
This gives us 665 numbers of 3 and a remainder of 2.
Step 4: Handling the Remainder. The remainder is 2, which is less than 3. Since 2 is a positive integer, we can include it
as is.
If the remainder were 1, it would be better to adjust one of the 3s and the remainder into two 2s because 3 + 1 = 2 + 2
and 3 × 1 = 3 < 2 × 2 = 4. However, in this case, the remainder is 2, so no adjustment is needed.
Step 5: Final Partition. Thus, the optimal partition is:
𝑛= 666 numbers: 665 threes and one two.
Step 6: Verification. Any other combination, such as using fewer threes and more twos or larger numbers, leads to a
product that is either equal or smaller. For example:
• Replacing a 3 and a 2 with two 2s and a 1 (but 1 reduces the product).
• Using larger numbers like 4, which can be expressed as 2 + 2, but the product remains the same (4 = 2 × 2).
Therefore, the maximum product is achieved with 665 threes and one two.
Final Answer. The positive integers are:
𝑛= 666
𝑎1 = 𝑎2 = · · · = 𝑎665 = 3
𝑎666 = 2
The maximum product is:
3665 × 2

always different letters.
Solution.
Understanding the Problem. We need to count the number of sequences of length 𝑛composed of the letters 𝑎, 𝑏, and 𝑐
such that:
(1) The sequence starts and ends with 𝑎.
(2) No two adjacent letters are the same.
Approach. We can model this problem using recurrence relations. Let’s define:
• 𝐴(𝑛): Number of valid sequences of length 𝑛ending with 𝑎.
• 𝐵(𝑛): Number of valid sequences of length 𝑛ending with 𝑏.
• 𝐶(𝑛): Number of valid sequences of length 𝑛ending with 𝑐.
Given the problem constraints, the first character must be 𝑎.
Base Case. For 𝑛= 1, the only valid sequence is 𝑎:
𝐴(1) = 1,
𝐵(1) = 𝐶(1) = 0
Recurrence Relations. For 𝑛> 1, the recurrence relations are:
𝐴(𝑛) = 𝐵(𝑛−1) + 𝐶(𝑛−1)
𝐵(𝑛) = 𝐴(𝑛−1) + 𝐶(𝑛−1)
𝐶(𝑛) = 𝐴(𝑛−1) + 𝐵(𝑛−1)

By symmetry, 𝐵(𝑛) = 𝐶(𝑛). Thus, we can simplify:
𝐴(𝑛) = 2𝐵(𝑛−1)
𝐵(𝑛) = 𝐴(𝑛−1) + 𝐵(𝑛−1)
Solving the Recurrence. We observe that 𝐴(𝑛) follows the recurrence:
𝐴(𝑛) = 𝐴(𝑛−1) + 2𝐴(𝑛−2)
The characteristic equation is:

with roots 𝑟= 2 and 𝑟= −1.
The general solution is:

Problem Statement. Find all positive integers 𝑐such that 𝑛(𝑛+ 𝑐) is never a perfect square for any positive integer 𝑛.
Solution. We need to find all positive integers 𝑐for which the expression 𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛is never a perfect square for any
positive integer 𝑛.
Initial Analysis. Consider the equation:
𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛= 𝑘2
for some integer 𝑘≥1. We can rewrite this as:
𝑘2 = 𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛
𝑘2 −𝑛2 = 𝑐𝑛
(𝑘−𝑛)(𝑘+ 𝑛) = 𝑐𝑛
Approach. For fixed 𝑐, we need to determine whether there exist positive integers 𝑛and 𝑘satisfying this equation.
Parametric Solution. Let us set 𝑘= 𝑛+ 𝑡where 𝑡is a non-negative integer. Substituting:
(𝑛+ 𝑡)2 = 𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛
𝑛2 + 2𝑡𝑛+ 𝑡2 = 𝑛2 + 𝑐𝑛
2𝑡𝑛+ 𝑡2 = 𝑐𝑛
𝑡2 = 𝑛(𝑐−2𝑡)
This implies that 𝑐−2𝑡must be a positive divisor of 𝑡2, and 𝑛=
𝑡2
𝑐−2𝑡must be a positive integer.
Special Cases.
Case 𝑐= 1. The equation becomes:
𝑡2 = 𝑛(1 −2𝑡)
For 𝑡≥1, the right side becomes non-positive while the left side is positive. Thus, the only possibility is 𝑡= 0, which
gives 𝑛= 0 - invalid. Therefore, no solutions exist for 𝑐= 1.
Case 𝑐= 2. The equation becomes:
𝑡2 = 𝑛(2 −2𝑡)
For 𝑡= 0: 𝑛= 0 (invalid) For 𝑡= 1: 1 = 𝑛(0) (no solution) For 𝑡≥1: The right side is non-positive. Thus, no solutions
exist for 𝑐= 2.
115

Case 𝑐= 4. The equation becomes:
𝑡2 = 𝑛(4 −2𝑡)
Testing small values:
• 𝑡= 0: 𝑛= 0 (invalid)
• 𝑡= 1: 1 = 𝑛(2) ⇒𝑛= 1
2 (not integer)
• 𝑡= 2: 4 = 𝑛(0) (no solution)
• 𝑡≥2: The denominator becomes zero or negative
Thus, no solutions exist for 𝑐= 4.
Verification of Other Values. For 𝑐= 3, we find a solution at 𝑛= 1:
1(1 + 3) = 4 = 22
Thus, 𝑐= 3 is excluded.
For 𝑐= 5, 𝑛= 4 gives:
4(4 + 5) = 36 = 62
Thus, 𝑐= 5 is excluded.
This pattern suggests that only 𝑐= 1, 2, 4 satisfy the condition.
General Proof. To prove that these are the only values, consider:
(1) For 𝑐≥5, we can often find solutions:
• For odd 𝑐, take 𝑛= (𝑐−1)2
4
• For even 𝑐, solutions frequently exist
(2) The cases 𝑐= 1, 2, 4 are special because:
• The equation 𝑡2 = 𝑛(𝑐−2𝑡) has no positive integer solutions
• The quadratic forms don’t permit perfect square solutions

2007 Session 2: 4 Correct Reasoning, Correct Final Answer.
Problem Statement. Let
𝑆= {5𝑎+ 503𝑏| 𝑎and 𝑏are nonnegative integers}.
What is the largest integer which does NOT belong to 𝑆?
Solution.
Understanding the Problem. The set 𝑆consists of all numbers expressible as 5𝑎+ 503𝑏where 𝑎and 𝑏are nonnegative
integers. We need to find the largest integer not in 𝑆.
Coin Problem Analogy. This problem resembles the Coin Problem, which seeks the largest monetary amount that
cannot be obtained using any combination of coins of given denominations. For two coprime integers 𝑥and 𝑦, the
116

largest number not expressible as 𝑚𝑥+ 𝑛𝑦is known as the Frobenius number, given by:
𝑔(𝑥,𝑦) = 𝑥𝑦−𝑥−𝑦.
Checking Coprimality. First, verify that gcd(5, 503) = 1. Since 503 is not divisible by 5, they are indeed coprime.
Applying the Frobenius Number Formula. Given that 5 and 503 are coprime, the largest number not in 𝑆is:
𝑔(5, 503) = 5 × 503 −5 −503 = 2515 −508 = 2007.
Verification. To ensure accuracy, let’s verify:
• Testing 2007: No nonnegative integers 𝑎,𝑏satisfy 5𝑎+ 503𝑏= 2007.
• Testing 2008: 5 × 301 + 503 × 1 = 1505 + 503 = 2008. Thus, 2008 is in 𝑆.
• Testing 2009: 5 × 100 + 503 × 3 = 500 + 1509 = 2009. Thus, 2009 is in 𝑆.
Conclusion. The largest integer not expressible as 5𝑎+ 503𝑏with nonnegative integers 𝑎,𝑏is indeed 2007 .
